01 · The company

What Abetworks is

Abet Works builds an integrated AI business suite for teams that sell, serve and scale — plus the data, AI and growth services to make it land.

Company snapshot

Founded: 2023
HQ: 301, VS Road, Mumbai, India
Founder & CEO: Vinayak Sonawane
Products: 9 in the suite
Service practices: 6
Email: admin@abetworks.in
Socials: X @abetworks · LinkedIn · Facebook

Started as a data, AI and digital services firm. Clients kept asking to license the internal tooling (lead capture, sub-minute routing, explainable scoring) — that became NuCRM, then the rest of the suite. The services practices still run alongside the software: they "build it, run it, sell it".

Reach & trust

  • Customers in India, GCC, Southeast Asia, UK, US
  • Interfaces in English, Hindi, Marathi; AI drafting in 30+ languages
  • Data residency: Mumbai (ap-south-1), Frankfurt, N. Virginia
  • ISO 27001-aligned controls · SOC 2 Type II programme
  • GDPR + India DPDP Act ready · signed DPA · sub-processor register
  • SSO/SAML + SCIM on Enterprise · 99.95% rolling uptime
  • Billing in USD or INR with GST · platform fees passed through at cost

The suite — 9 products, one data model, one login, one bill

AI CRM & pipeline management. Scores every lead in <30s, drafts the next touch, shows where the pipeline leaks.

Consolidation pitch: a typical point-tool stack costs ≈$318/user/month across 7 subscriptions; the Abetworks suite starts at ≈$142/user/month — one contract, one support team, no per-task or per-viewer billing.

02 · What you wrote down as the goal

Your goal, exactly as documented

Three documents define it: the website README (June 2026), the master plan + BlueRails report in vinayakss007/abetworks-modern-site, and the owner-set mission list in your local workspace (Oct 2026).

🎯 From the README: the website goal

"Build a world-class website (like Apple, Stripe, Linear) that showcases all 7+ SaaS products, presents agency services professionally, integrates blog and 100+ free tools, scores 95/100 on BlueRails AI Visibility (from 69), and converts visitors into trial signups, demo requests and leads."

Target hosting: Vercel/Netlify · Framework: Next.js · AI readiness 20 → 100/100.

📦 From the owner mission list: the product goal

"Every repo = sellable MVP (live SaaS with subscriptions), compatible with nuCRM, yet fully standalone. Order: 2 public repos first, then the rest as they're made public. Only work on PUBLIC repos. Private ones must be made public first."

Flagship: NuCRM Enterprise v2 (multi-tenant, 224 tables, Postgres) — the hub every other app must integrate with over HTTP.

The scoreboard you must move: BlueRails 69 → 95

The three insights that matter

  • "AI isn't finding you unprompted" — zero organic appearances in open-ended AI searches. That's where new-to-AI brands start, and it's 0.
  • "You own more than half the AI conversation in your category" — when AI is asked directly, Abet Works shows up 75% of the time. Brand is strong.
  • "AI talks about you but doesn't link to you" — 28/100 citations. A citation is the difference between awareness and traffic.

Fresh probe — 16 Oct 2026: the live Render site answers on /, /pricing/, /products/, /robots.txt and /sitemap.xml (all 200) but /llms.txt is 404 — the AI navigation file doesn't exist yet. Its robots.txt already allows Googlebot, Bingbot, DuckDuckBot and Applebot, and its Host, Sitemap and og:url metadata all point at https://abetworks.in — the site is pre-configured for the real domain, which still returns 520. Domain pulse: tools.abetworks.in 200 · abetworks.in 520 · www.abetworks.in 520 · hub.abetworks.in no connection · leadworks.abetworks.in 520.
Root cause of the 520 (found 16 Oct): abetworks.in sits on Cloudflare and CF can't reach a valid origin — while the site itself is healthy (abetworks-website.onrender.com = 200, last build 31 Jul 2026). A request to Render with Host: abetworks.in answers 403: the custom domain was never attached to the service. Two-sided fix, ~5 minutes: 1. Render → service abetworks-website → Custom Domains → add abetworks.in + www · 2. Cloudflare DNS → point @/www at the Render target, proxied, SSL Full (strict). That one move flips 448 sitemap URLs + every canonical + every og:image from dead to live. Full playbook: ai-visibility-fixpack.md §F1.
Smoke-sweep evidence: the DNS records already exist (apex, www and the subdomain wildcard) but still target the legacy InfinityFree host — subdomain /robots.txt 302s to errors.infinityfree.net and the apex /robots.txt is a 30-day-cached file from that host. Re-point, attach, purge cache = live.
Deploy blocker — resolved and merged (16 Oct): every app ran the dev-only SQLite path, and web-service disks on Render are ephemeral — SQLite there means all customer data is wiped on every deploy or restart. The switch is now on every main: 7 Postgres PRs merged (abetanalytics #12 · abetagent #13 · abetflow #11 · leadgenius #14 · leadworks #13 · abetConnect #15 · abetapi #12) — fresh Postgres migration history, prisma migrate deploy on every boot, and every generated migration applied to a live PostgreSQL 16 before merge. What's left is a click, not code: the one-click fleet blueprint (render.blueprint.fleet.yaml, PR aw-deploy#3) provisions 7 services + 7 Postgres + 5 Key Value + 7 custom domains, fully wired, ≈$91/mo production / $0 trial profile stated upfront. Then the §F1 DNS fix + cache purge. Click-by-click: deploy-runbook.md — and since PR aw-deploy#6, the repo itself carries the same playbook (deploy/DEPLOY.md + deploy/STRIPE.md + deploy/smoke-test.sh); the old 36-service instructions are gone.

The analytics answer to “why?” — 18 Oct (2 PRs MERGED): the flagship now explains its own number. POST /api/metrics/{id}/breakdown splits a metric’s movement between two adjacent ranges across one dimension (named, or the highest-coverage one), and every segment is computed by the same engine that produced the headline — so the parts cannot drift from the whole. Only the metric’s own events can own a segment, and events that lack the chosen dimension share a (not set) bucket, so additive parts add back exactly and an unrelated event type never prints a zero row. The dashboard gets a per-metric Breakdown action that runs through a session-authenticated server action (no API key in the browser). The demo’s last step answers the board’s follow-up: the frozen 3,820 week split by channel — Instagram +750, Google +650, Email −180 against the week before. PRs: aw-abetanalytics#18 (API + dashboard action; 258/258 tests, live end-to-end on :3600) · aw-deploy#18 MERGED (demo STEP 8; every delivery signature-verified). The one-pager’s analytics proof now cites exactly what the demo prints.
Correctness, same day: a range holding more than 50,000 events used to be answered from the first 50,000 rows — a number that is not the range’s metric, with nothing in the response saying so. #19 fetches one row past the bound and answers 422 (withAuth maps the typed error; preview now shares the loader and refuses backwards ranges); #18 refuses a backwards breakdown window too. Measured on real Postgres + the built app: 50,001 → preview 422 / snapshot 422, 50,000 → preview 200 (value=50000) / snapshot 201, backwards → 422.
The sales demo stopped lying about its database — 18 Oct (PR aw-deploy#20): Prisma 7's CLI no longer loads .env, but the Next app does. Run exactly as the README says — create the app's .env, export nothing — and the runner booted the app against the configured database while minting its API key with tsx against the fallback one. Every step then answered 401, and the script still exited 0: a demo that looks like a success while proving nothing. with_app_env now sources each app's .env/.env.local for seed, seed:key and db:seed (main app, Flow, Connect, Agent), so the key and the app share one database. Measured with no DATABASE_URL exported: before 401/401/401; after 201/201, snapshot 3,820, 4 deliveries both-signature-verified, healthy rule silent. Next: the demo scripts should assert their own statuses — a 401 must fail the run, not print.
The frozen number can finally leave the building — 18 Oct (3 PRs MERGED): AbetAnalytics sells one thing — the number the board agreed, frozen with the definition it was computed from — and its whole reason to exist is that someone outside the workspace has to see it. There was no way to hand it over. The dashboard now mints a 192-bit capability token and serves a keyless read-only page at /s/{token}: the value, the range and the definition as-run (name, kind, event/field/filters/steps), read from the snapshot row alone — so renaming or reconfiguring the metric afterwards cannot change what the page says. It is noindex (the link is the capability, not a public post), and revocation is a single filter — findLiveSharedSnapshot reads shareRevokedAt: null — so Stop kills the URL and sharing again mints a fresh token that retires the old one. API-first buyers get the same: POST /api/snapshots/{id}/share (idempotent — 201 the first time, 200 after, same URL) and DELETE /api/snapshots/{id}/share, both calling one lib so the two doors cannot drift; a foreign snapshot answers 404, exactly like a missing one. Measured on the built app + real Postgres: keyless /s/{token} 200 with the frozen 3,820; no key 401, unknown 404; revoke 200 → the same link 404; rotate → new 200, old 404. PRs: aw-abetanalytics#21 (dashboard + page; 272/272 tests) · #22 (API + one implementation) · aw-deploy#22 (demo STEP 9 — the analytics demo's only PASS/FAIL step, 10/10). The weekly number arrives on its own — 20 Oct (2 PRs MERGED): a number that has to be fetched is a number that gets forgotten, and a "weekly number" product that only works when you remember to open a dashboard is a subscription people cancel in month two. AbetAnalytics now emails the frozen number: the same metric, over the same window, frozen with its definition, with the same revocable read-only link the dashboard hands out — so the emailed value and the value anyone opens later cannot drift apart. Four decisions carry the weight: a report never re-pages (the alert rules are not evaluated for a report-generated snapshot — the customer may already have been paged for that number); the slot is a wall-clock time in a named zone ("every Monday at 08:00" in Europe/Berlin stays at 08:00 local across the daylight-saving change — the UTC instant moves, the promise does not) and a schedule created after its slot waits for the next one; the schedule owns the idempotency, not Redis (lastPeriod is the occurrence already delivered, compared on the row, so a restart, a second instance or a retry cannot send the same week twice — and a failed send is retried against the same window, never a newer one); the tick is an hourly BullMQ job registered on boot, so a report does not need anyone to restart the app to arrive. Measured on the built app + real Postgres + real Redis + a real HTTP mail sink: tick → worker → email delivered → the link opens keyless 200 → a second tick sends nothing → a disabled schedule sends nothing → send-now delivers without consuming the weekly slot → delete unsubscribes; bad email 400, bad day 400, unknown zone 422, no key 401, foreign schedule 404. PRs: aw-abetanalytics#32 (322/322 tests, 19 new — the DST boundary, a half-hour zone, a schedule created after its slot, an already-delivered occurrence, a failed attempt retried then given up on) · aw-deploy#43 (demo STEP 10, 12/12 — the email read back out of the demo's own capture server, a real HTTP delivery rather than a log line).
The hub's promise is finally kept by the product — 20 Oct (2 PRs MERGED): every free tool on the hub ends with a capture card that says "one email with your report — no newsletter, no spam", and the privacy note under it says the same — and nothing sent it. The lead went to disk, the owner was notified, and the report the visitor had just been shown was never emailed: a promise the product could not keep, told in the conversion copy on the only surface that faces a buyer. Now each free tool publishes the result it is already showing (title, headline, label/value rows, one fix tip) and the card sends it with the address. Two things make that safe rather than naive: the server reduces the report before it is allowed near an email (strings cut to a length that fits, the row count capped, non-strings dropped rather than stringified, HTML escaped rather than trusted), so a visitor cannot turn "send me my report" into a mail cannon; and the transport says which channel ran — http when a provider took the message, log when there is no provider and it went to the server log — so the card says "Report sent ✓" only for a real delivery and otherwise says plainly that the report was not emailed. Measured on the built app against a live HTTP mail sink: a lead with a report → 200 delivered:true and the message delivered (the score, both failing rows, the fix tip, the tool link); the same lead on disk carries the report; a lead without a report → captured, delivered:false, nothing sent; a bad address 400; with no provider → delivered:false, no delivery, and the message in the server log saying it was not delivered. Create one via POST /api/metrics" — is advice for somebody who already writes code. The dashboard now offers one click: POST /api/example-data loads a real week — two metrics, 223 events, 3 frozen snapshots — written through the same tables the API writes, so the number on the screen is computed by the same engine that computes a customer's number. From there the breakdown by channel, the read-only share link, the weekly report and the "what changed?" panel all work on it. Three rules keep it example data and nothing else: it is labelled (Example: …, plus a dashboard banner), the removal is surgical (the two example_* event types and the two example metrics, by id — a customer's own events are never touched, and their workspace is empty again afterwards), and it is idempotent (a second call changes nothing, so a double-click cannot double the events). The door takes a session or an API key: the prospect who signs up on the web and the prospect who integrates the API are the same buyer, and neither should wait for the other to see the product work. The site repo is identified — and the AI pack was rewritten because it would have published lies (20 Oct): the live marketing site is abetworks-website → abetworks-website.vercel.app — a static HTML services site (services/*.html, a blog page, a company page), not a Next.js app: no /products/, no prices anywhere. The prepared pack would have made that worse: it shipped one llms.txt for one assumed site, naming nine /products/<slug>/ pages and a price for each — none of which this site has — so applying it unchanged would have published nine dead product links and a price list the site does not state: an AI-visibility regression dressed as a fix. So the pack was made site-aware and self-verifying: the page list is explicit (every omission carries its reason), the generator refuses to run if the list names a page the repository does not have, and it prints the pages that exist but are not listed — so a link the site does not have cannot be published, and a page left out is a visible decision. aw-leadworks main was red — and the cause was a stack merged without its base (21 Oct): the validator still carried its own copy of namedLeads and hand-wrote the id: { in: … } clause, while lib/lead-crowd.ts — the module that exists to own both — sat beside it with a test pinning that no module on the job path may write its own. The copy came in with the lead-crowd work, whose branch was stacked on the still-open enrichment branch: merging the top of that stack without its base brought the base's older validator.ts with it — and the base's copy fails the top's own test. Fixed by making the validator read the shared module and state the query once (crowdWhere({ workspaceId: job.workspaceId }, namedLeads(config.leadIds)) — the workspace filter rides inside crowdWhere, so a named crowd can never widen the query), and by moving the stale test with it. 493/493, tsc + eslint + build clean, main green again. The lesson recorded for the queue: a stacked branch merged alone carries its base's files, so the top's own tests are the ones that notice.

A sweep for the paths that WRITE — because the new features had no production proof (21 Oct): acceptance.sh is deliberately read-only (no secrets, no writes) so it can be pointed at production at any moment — but the features that matter most to a buyer are all writes (the first run, the share link, the weekly report), and "it worked on my box" is not "it works where the customers are". So deploy/acceptance/acceptance-writes.sh takes a key and, per app, exercises: the first run (loads; the removal takes everything away again), the share link (mints → opens with no account at all → revokes → the revoked link is 404), and the weekly report (schedules → cancels) — and everything it creates it removes: the last check reads the app's own list back and insists it is empty, so a sweep can never leave a customer with example data in their workspace. An app with no key is skipped, honestly, not failed. Measured against the three apps live on the box with real keys: 15 passed · 0 failed · 0 skipped. The sweep caught two things about itself while it was being written, both only visible when it runs against something real: the share endpoint takes a snapshot's id and not a metric's (the app was right, saying 404 rather than 500; the sweep was wrong), and the link's absolute origin is the app's own NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL, so the sweep probes the link's path on the host it is sweeping and reports the configured origin rather than assuming it. DEPLOY.md now runs it as part of the walk, after the read-only sweep. PR: aw-deploy#57.

The A/B split is one number, checked in all three of its places — 20 Oct (PRs aw-leadgenius#50 · aw-deploy#47): ABTest.trafficSplit was a JSON copy of the variant rows' weights — written at create, served to the buyer by GET /api/campaigns/:id, and consulted by nothing in the code, while the number that actually decides an arm is ABTestVariant.weight (assignVariant buckets sha256(test:contact) % total). Nothing compared the copy to the arms, and nothing compared the arms to the cohorts that arrived — so a campaign's own detail page could report any split at all, and a winner could be named on two audiences of different size. src/lib/traffic-split.ts now holds that arithmetic once: the create route writes the column through declareSplit, the same function the verdict door reads it back through; a copy that is not an object of arm→percentage, that names an arm no variant carries, or that disagrees with the weights the rows hold is 409 quoting both spellings; and a winner whose arm sits further from its declared share than chance explains is 409 on a per-arm one-vs-rest z against the split's own denominator, alarming at 3.290527 (two-sided 0.1 %, verified in the unit tests by Simpson integration of the normal density to ~1e-9) with a five-expected-per-side floor so a handful of contacts is never lectured. The declaration is judged before the cohorts — a broken copy must never be reported as an unlucky audience — and both refusals fire only when a winner is named, so complete without a winner stays the remedy. Measured live on the built app + real Postgres + real Redis + real SMTP (step [27]): twelve contacts enrolled into one arm behind a 100/0 window deliver twelve mails and none to the other arm, and the verdict is refused with its own arithmetic out loud — "declared 50 of 100 (50.0%) and received 12 of 12 enrollments where 6.0 were expected — z=+3.46 against the rest of the traffic" — while the refused test still completes 200 with winningVariant and confidence both null; a second test enrolled by its own hash to ledger=1 margin=2 measures |z|=0.58, both arms really send, and is allowed its verdict with a numeric confidence; the run closes on {"copies":"0","totals":"0"} — no stored split disagrees with its arms, no weights fail to sum to 100. 398/398 demo checks, 704/704 unit tests (43 new), build + lint clean. Not claimed: assignment never moved (the rows always were operative), no door edits trafficSplit, and the check cannot tell a genuine audience skew from a test that simply never filled its arms.
A rule that says when it applies now means it — 20 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#32 · aw-deploy#48): EnrichmentRule.conditions is commented // When to apply this rule and RULE_CREATE makes it required — an operator cannot create a rule without saying when it applies — and nothing in the app read it back. Three doors picked leads instead, each carrying its own hardcoded status in NEW|ENRICHED: the minute that turns a SCHEDULED rule into a job (scheduler.ts), the worker that decides which rows to write (getLeadsToEnrich), and the third copy (runScheduledEnrichment). So the seeded rule Auto-enrich new leads ({ status: "NEW" }) selected exactly the same crowd as { status: "CONVERTED" } would: required, filled in, and decoration. src/lib/rule-conditions.ts holds the declaration once now — conditionsProblem refuses it naming every problem at once, normalizeConditions is what gets stored, leadWhere is the one SQL translation all three doors ask for (so the minute that queues and the worker that runs cannot pick different crowds), leadMatches re-applies the same declaration to every row the query fetched — filtering, so it can only narrow what is written to a person — and describeConditions puts the run's answer in words: appliesTo: "seniority VP, tagged aim-vq3ew". Two rules keep this from widening: a rule that declares nothing keeps NEW|ENRICHED, not the whole workspace, so the contacted and the rejected stay alone as they were; and a stored blob that cannot be read selects nobody — the schedule skips it and logs why, ?action=run answers 400, a job carrying one enriches zero rows. Junk in a column that gates writes to a person is not a licence to fall back to everything. The comparison is character for character, and measured, not assumed: the first draft upper-cased tag conditions the way it upper-cases the two enums, and against this workspace's own leads (["vip","dubai"]) that was wrong immediately — array_contains on vip returned 2 rows, on VIP returned 0, because a Json column compares JSON values. A folded tag would have been a stored rule the query cannot honour.
On the call (aw-deploy#48, STEP 9 of demo/run-demo.sh leadworks): two of the run's own leads take a tag only they carry, and the worker's per-lead counters become the measure. Three declarations are 400 at the door — a key no Lead column spells ("department" is not an attribute a rule can condition on — the supported keys are status, seniority, score, tags), a status the column does not hold (the refusal quotes the six words schema.prisma spells), a score range no number can satisfy (at least 90 and at most 40) — and four accepted rules visit 2, 2, 0 and 0 leads: the tag alone, the tag AND seniority VP, the tag AND seniority IC (an unsatisfiable conjunction visits none), and the same tag written in capitals, which visits none. The workspace holds 24 NEW leads while the aimed rule reaches 2 — a declaration narrows, never widens. 18 assertions ✓ / 0 ✗, exit 0, run twice (13 of them from this step), and the run removes its leads and its rules. 371/371 unit tests on the product (46 new, including the source-order guards that pin the doors' wiring and a repo-wide walk asserting status: { in: ["NEW","ENRICHED"] } appears in no .ts under src/ any more), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors, build clean.
Not claimed: the step measures reach, not enrichment — each visit still ends at a provider stub whose write is refused, which is why a visited lead lands in failedItems (processed=0, failed=2) rather than a changed row, and the demo says so in its own printout; actions — what the rule says to enrich — is still read by nothing; POST /api/leads/{id}?action=enrich still queues a workspace-wide job that ignores the id; and EnrichmentLog.workspaceId is still written as "".
The enrichment visit writes what the row lacks — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#33 · aw-deploy#51): #32 proved which leads a rule reaches; this is the write that follows them, and it was broken in a way no counter showed. enrichLead returned the providers' answers plus a confidence number and updateLeadEnrichment spread it straight into prisma.lead.update — Lead has no confidence column, so Prisma refused the whole update. Measured on the running app: a rule aimed at two leads finished processed=0, failed=2 with both rows untouched, lastEnrichedAt still null. Enrichment, the feature on the front of the sales page, wrote nothing for anybody while reporting itself as failing. Three more lies sat behind it: the same line computed score: calculateLeadScore(result) from the provider's return value, so had the update landed a lead whose real signals earned 82 would have read score: 5; EnrichmentLog.workspaceId was the literal "" (the FK would have refused it too) with status hardcoded SUCCESS; and the six "providers" answered with invented data — industry: "SaaS", companySize: "51-200", revenue: "$10M-$50M" — which is worse than silence, because the fiction lands in a customer's row and reads like research.
src/lib/enrich-plan.ts owns the write now, and all four of its rules are testable offline: only a column Lead actually has, and only into a gap — a value a person typed, imported or corrected outlives anything a provider guessed, so enrichment is additive and running it twice changes nothing the first run did; a value whose shape the column cannot hold (a bare string where technologies is a list of words) is dropped, not coerced, while the log still carries what the provider said; score is never written — the number a buyer sorts the list by is not a byproduct of a call to a third party; and status moves one way only, NEW → ENRICHED, the schema's own word for it and the only code in the product that earns it, and only when a field actually landed, so a contacted or converted lead is never pulled back by a background job. A visit that learned nothing still stamps lastEnrichedAt and logs PARTIAL — the true state of every provider in this build except derive, and a log line claiming SUCCESS would have been the second way this job lied.
derive is the provider that makes the whole path provable without an API key: src/lib/seniority.ts reads the seniority a person's own title states. It abstains — a title outside the table returns null, never UNKNOWN, because that word is a judgment someone makes about a person and a lookup filing it as a default would record an opinion as a fact — and it matches phrases before words, because Vice President contains President, Managing Director contains Director and General Manager contains Manager, each at a different rung than the title means. Head of Growth, Team Lead, Principal, Architect, EVP and Chief of Staff have no entry on purpose: they mean different rungs at different companies. The six call-site names are dispatched and answer nothing until a key is configured. And a rule listing "hubspot" used to be stored, read by the worker and answered with silence — the rule sat active and enriched nobody, forever, looking like a quiet week; both doors now refuse it by name with the list the worker dispatches on, and a rule typed Apollo is stored as apollo so it reaches its case. The provider list and the switch are checked against each other in both directions from the source, and every writable column is read back out of prisma/schema.prisma, so neither list can drift.
On the call (aw-deploy#51, STEP 10 of demo/run-demo.sh leadworks): three leads arrive the way the buyer's CSV rows do — a title, nothing under it — each carrying a score of 82 a person set. One run of a rule listing derive reaches all three (processed=3, failed=0): the Managing Director reads C_LEVEL and ENRICHED — not DIRECTOR, the word inside it — the VP a rep typed on the Product Manager survives, and Head of Growth gains nothing and is not filed UNKNOWN; all three rows carry the visit's stamp, all three scores are still 82, and the rule's own log — read back through GET /api/enrichment-rules/{id} — shows one SUCCESS with fieldsEnriched: ["seniority"] beside two PARTIAL with empty lists, every row naming the workspace it happened in. A second run reaches the same three leads and writes no field at all. 21 assertions from this step ✓ / 0 ✗, 38 across the demo, exit 0, and the run removes its leads and its rules (leads=15 rules=0 logs=0 after it — deleting a lead cascades its enrichment log). 413/413 unit tests on the product (42 new), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors (16 pre-existing warnings), build clean.
Not claimed: five of the seven provider names still go nowhere — dispatched, answering nothing until a key and an egress route exist; score still has no owner (validator.ts decrements it on a failed check and the sales process sets it by hand, so recomputing it from the merged row is its own round, and this one deliberately writes none of it); actions — what the rule says to enrich — is still read by nothing; and POST /api/leads/{id}?action=enrich still queues a workspace-wide job that ignores the id.
Every lead’s 0–100 is arithmetic now, and the row carries its work — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#34 MERGED as 6494801 · aw-leadworks#35 MERGED as 768d315 · aw-deploy#51): Lead.score had a column, a sort order (?orderBy=score desc) and a sentence in the landing README since the first migration, and no producer: intake left it at 0, the CSV importer left it at 0, the validator subtracted 10 per failed check from whatever the column held — always from 0, so it could only ever stay 0 — and a caller could simply PATCH {score: 9999} for a 200 that put that lead first for every reader of the list. Measured: the demo’s three best-imported contacts sat at score=0 holding an email, a company, a title, a location, a country and an industry. src/lib/lead-score.ts owns it now: 17 published terms over 5 groups that sum to exactly 100 (contact 30 · identity 16 · company 36 · signals 14 · provenance 4), so “0–100” is arithmetic rather than a clamp; presence earns and format does not; the number says how much the workspace knows — UNKNOWN earns 0 while IC and C_LEVEL both earn 6, and status and tags earn nothing. A number a person types is an input, not a write: PATCH {score: 82} lands in scoreOverride and keeps the row’s own total beside it in scoreBreakdown + scoredAt (three added columns, migration 20261021120000_lead_score_owner), score: null hands it back, and POST /api/leads declares no score at all. The validator’s −10 became a priced rule, so the same job run twice cannot decay a row twice, and VALIDATE gained leadIds because it used to walk — and re-price, or delete — every lead in the workspace on the strength of one POST. The cadence an operator types is the cadence the sweep reads — 22 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#49 OPEN at 3fac942 + 7d58c36 + 2e362e4, stacked on #48 · aw-deploy#64 OPEN at 62c0ed1 + 5080e8d, stacked on #62): runScheduledJobs decided whether a stored cadence meant now with shouldRunNow — nineteen lines at the foot of scheduler.ts, under a comment that read Simplified cron matching - in production use croner or similar: split each field on commas, compare whole strings, and let anything with five-or-more fields through. Measured against the running app: four sources created through POST /api/sources in one minute and left four sweep minutes — */5 * * * * made 0 jobs in a window holding two of the fifth-minutes, 0 9 * * 1-5 0 forever, and * * * * * 1 4 — one per minute, on every day, because a six-field expression was read as its first five fields and the one meant to restrict the row to Mondays was the field that got dropped; the control * * * * * made 4, which is what proves the other three were a deaf sweep, not a dead one. Nothing was printed in any of the three cases. The same three strings sent to POST /api/enrichment-rules came back 400 each quoting the offending part — out of a second copy of the same grammar sitting in rule-actions.ts. One product, two verdicts on one string, and the door that checked was the door that had never been wrong about its matcher and always wrong about the product.
One module now spells the grammar and the ranges. src/lib/cron-schedule.ts is 195 lines with zero imports: SCHEDULE_FIELDS is the only place minute 0-59, hour 0-23, day-of-month 1-31, month 1-12 and day-of-week 0-7 are written down (with 7 folded to 0 before matching, because both are Sunday in two living traditions), GRAMMAR quotes the accepted shapes once so a refusal cannot drift from what is accepted, scheduleProblem returns every field’s problem in one message naming the refused part in quotes and the field it belongs to — and refuses by name what this build will not read: a weekday or month name (naming the convention it comes from), ?, #, L, W, a step of 0, a backwards range, a number outside its field, an empty entry between commas, anything that is not exactly five fields — and matchesSchedule honours *, a whole number, a comma list, an inclusive range and a step (*/n, a-b/n, a/n) while answering false, never throwing, on anything scheduleProblem would refuse. shouldRunNow is gone rather than shadowed — a test greps all of src/ for the function and its comment — all three row-shaped legs of the sweep call the one matcher with no getX() clock read of their own, and each asks the grammar per row first: an unreadable cadence prints Source <id> is active and scheduled, but no minute matches it — <reason> and skips, which is the only half of this that reaches a row stored before any door could refuse it. rule-actions.ts imports the table instead of re-spelling it, so the rule door’s 400 contains the matcher’s sentence verbatim (the demo asserts containment, not similarity), and POST /api/sources + PATCH /api/sources/{id} refuse an unreadable cadence before their writes, gated on typeof … === "string" so null stays a clear rather than a cadence to refuse.

NO_DENOMINATOR is the words for a share with no denominator: { measured: false, percent: 0 }, which is neither 100% of nothing nor a 0% that claims nothing was done — an export whose filter matched nothing finishes COMPLETED and reports not measured. countersProblem reads “16 rows accounted for against a denominator of 0” out of a row that cannot be true, and GET /api/jobs/{id} hands the whole reading back beside the row: measured, percent, reason, verdict, verdictNote, problem, results: { recorded, returned, truncated } — recorded counted by the database, because results is take: 50 and 50-of-50 is not 50-of-4,000.
The records are written where an outcome is decided. ENRICH, VALIDATE and DEDUPE each decide something per lead and threw the decision away, keeping three integers; now every row they reached has a JobResult — the visit that filled a column is SUCCESS naming the columns, the visit that reached a row and wrote nothing is SKIPPED naming which gate of planEnrichmentWrite stopped it — no column in the table for it, the row already held a value, or the value is not a shape the column takes — read from enrich-plan’s own holds and ENRICH_WRITABLE rather than decided a second time, with both the columns the providers named and the columns actions.fields let through, since the narrowed set alone cannot tell a refusal from silence, the lead that threw is FAILED with the message, the duplicate folded into a master names both ids. Two orders are pinned. A record exists before the row says it finished: each insert is inside the loop, next to the lead write it describes, so a COMPLETED row cannot name results that were never stored, and a job that throws halfway leaves the records for the rows it did finish. A removal is recorded before the row it names is deleted: JobResult.leadId is ON DELETE SET NULL, so VALIDATE action=remove and DEDUPE merge insert first with the id copied into the payload, and what survives is a record pointing at nothing that still says which lead it described — writing either one after the merge would be a foreign-key violation dressed up as a log line.

A cancelled job stops — the mark an operator makes is obeyed by the worker that is running, 22 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#60 OPEN at a1f42e5, a direct child of main, which already carries #59 (merged as 7f02bb7) · aw-deploy#78 OPEN at 910a291, likewise a direct child of main, which already carries #76 (landed as #77, 4433204) : the API list has carried POST /api/jobs/{id}?action=cancel since the first commit, and the table answered what it had ever done: 475 Job rows, 466 COMPLETED, 0 CANCELLED. src/lib/job-stop.ts owns the mark now: MARK, isMarked (a row that is gone is not a mark — absence is not permission to write a terminal status to a row that is not there), startWrite, and stoppedWrite — the door's own word beside the denominator the worker walked, its counters, and the percentage between them, so a job stopped at 4 of 16 says 25 and cannot say 100 about a run it stopped. ENRICH, VALIDATE and DEDUPE re-read the status at the boundary before the next row: the row already in flight finishes, the next one is never started — which is the whole of what a stop cannot promise, and why the check sits at a boundary rather than at an await. The reading stayed in lib/job-report.ts, one owner for the words: stopAt splits a stopped row into reached and left, because the counters alone cannot tell never tried from tried and could not; countersProblem forgives exactly one shape — a denominator with nothing accounted for under a row that says CANCELLED is what a mark read before the first row looks like, and calling it “finished without saying it visited any row” would accuse a stopped job of the one thing the stop guarantees it did not do — while the same numbers on a COMPLETED row are still an accusation, and a mark is no licence to smooth over-reporting. enrichment.completed is dispatched only when the walk was not stopped: there is no fourth event name to invent, because an event for a job the operator stopped is the wire version of a typed 100.
The mark outranks every word a worker writes, at all four places a status is stamped. The claim is one updateMany with status: { not: MARK } in its WHERE, so a cancel landing between the worker's snapshot read and its RUNNING write is not answered by a status the worker made up — count: 0 means never begin, and the row keeps the operator's word with no startedAt and nothing visited. Enrichment's per-lead stamp is guarded the same way and now carries totalItems too, so a row read between two leads is a measurable share rather than a bare status. The finish writes where: { status: stopped ? MARK : { not: MARK } } — a stop under the mark, a finish around it — and a finish that matched nothing is the row saying the mark, repaired with the counters this worker actually holds. A throw cannot stamp FAILED over it either.

The queue says no in two voices, and the row it refused has to say which one it heard — 24 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#77 OPEN at c0209ab + d5bd624, a direct child of main at 4a88f17 · aw-deploy#96 OPEN at 749dc5f, a direct child of its main at 79649bf): seven doors insert a Job and hand it to the queue, and all seven caught QueueUnavailableError to answer 503 with “the work is saved, retry shortly”. Measured on a running instance with its own Redis turned off, both halves were false: every door that writes a row answered 500 with a 0-byte body and left the row PENDING, error: null — the export door leaving a PENDING Job and a PENDING Export, the lead door leaving totalItems: 1 beside a report that said the job finished; seven retries of refused rows minted seven more PENDING rows; and after 150 s of a healthy queue 11 rows were still PENDING with waiting at 0. Not stale — trapped: #42’s retryAsk refuses a PENDING row with 409 and “it will run”, so no door would resume one, and the export’s own retry door answered 400 “Can only retry failed exports”. The only thing that moved a row was a raw PATCH {status:"FAILED"}.
Why the 503 never arrived took a diagnostic printed from inside a door’s own catch, because the queue refuses twice. A Redis that dies under a running process rejects in ~0.1 s with Stream isn’t writeable and enableOfflineQueue options is false — one of six shapes the classifier knew, none of them this — and the driver’s AggregateError has an empty message with its connect ECONNREFUSED lines on .errors[]. A process that starts while Redis is already gone does not reject at all: queue.add waits for a ready connection that never comes, and that door was measured hung past three minutes, producing no bytes at any code.
Three decisions and one ordering close it. isConnectionFailure reads the message and walks errors[] to a bounded depth; enqueue races the hand-off against HANDOFF_DEADLINE_MS = 3000 and turns the expiry into the same refusal; and src/lib/queue-refusal.ts writes it — FAILED + completedAt + error starting never queued: — by updateMany guarded on status: "PENDING", so a worker that took the row in that window is never answered on top of. All seven doors mark before they answer and put jobId in the 503; both export doors move the Export too; and the four hand-offs with no caller in front of them — the minute’s three sweep legs and the bulk enrich run — refuse through enqueueOrMark and keep the tick alive, which is the eleven-row shape itself. Enqueue-before-insert (#39’s scrape shape) was rejected and disclosed: the payload names the row a worker reads.

A row the queue never took did not finish, and the job report stops saying it did — 25 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#80 OPEN at 8f26df4 + 0859b86 + 6618cc0, a direct child of main at 65bd942): GET /api/jobs/{id} reads a Job row and hands back a report beside it — the share of the crowd the job walked, the verdict the status column cannot hold, and since #40 a problem naming the case where the row’s own numbers cannot all be true at once. #40 wrote one rule about that last key and applied it to every row: a denominator with nothing accounted for under it was “this job finished without saying it visited any row”. True of a COMPLETED row written that way. Not true of the row a refused hand-off leaves behind. Measured on this round’s own runtime first. Base origin/main at 65bd942, built in the worktree, three instances over the session’s own database leadworks_r52 (cloned from the leadworks_r51 dump): :3323 with REDIS_URL on a port nothing listens on, :3326 on a private Redis, and :3327 carrying main’s src/lib/job-report.ts byte for byte, so both readings could be pressed against the same rows in the same table (/tmp/opencode/r52_measure.log, r52_after.log, r52_ab.log). The door that leaves the row is POST /api/leads/{id}?action=enrich: it writes the Job first, denominator in the insert (totalItems: 1, src/app/api/leads/[id]/route.ts:85; the scheduled sweep does the same with up to 100 at scheduler.ts:223, a rule run at enrichment.ts:340), and hands off after. With no queue to take it the door answered 30.0 s and nothing at all (curl exit 000 — that half is #50’s open deadline; this branch touches no queue code), and the row it left read status PENDING, totalItems 1, processedItems 0, progress 0, startedAt null, error null beside a report calling it finished.
Three shapes, one false tense. The same counters under the status a worker writes when it takes a row printed the identical sentence — RUNNING beside 1 promised and 0 visited is a job that has not reached its first row, not one that got through it silently — and the open row with no denominator (what POST /api/jobs leaves) said “this job stamped no denominator”, #40’s past tense about a row that has no past. So the reading asks the row what phase it is in before it accuses it of anything: phaseOf(status) partitions the five words Job.status holds — PENDING/RUNNING are open (a worker may still count rows into this row), CANCELLED is stopped (#40’s forgiven shape, under a name that says why it is forgiven), COMPLETED/FAILED and any word the schema does not know are closed. jobReport derives the phase once, from the column, and gives it to the two readings that can be wrong about it: countersProblem keeps its done > total contradiction for every phase — rows accounted for come from a worker that reached them, and no phase explains 14 of 10 — and fires the silent-finish sentence only on a closed row; progressOf keeps measured: false and percent: 0 for an open row with no denominator and changes only which of the two “not measured: …” sentences explains it (NOT_STARTED beside NO_DENOMINATOR). Telling the truth takes problem away, and problem was the only key a caller could watch, so the sentence moves rather than disappearing: it arrives in verdictNote, the key that was null beside it.
The matched pair, both legs on one row. PENDING/1/0 — base calls it finished at 618 bytes, this build answers not started: a denominator of 1 with nothing counted against it, and no worker has written a start on this row — the wait is the fact, not a result at 667 bytes; RUNNING/1/0 — in flight … what a worker that has not reached its first row looks like; PENDING with no denominator — no worker has written on this row, so it has stamped no denominator … yet. And the four shapes that had to stay put, identical on both legs: a hand-written COMPLETED with 12 promised and 0 visited still prints #40’s accusation byte for byte, 626 = 626; the 386-row contradiction (16 row(s) accounted for against a denominator of 0) unchanged; a CANCELLED row’s the mark was read before the worker reached a row unchanged; and the reference leg’s own row — :3326’s door answered 200 in 133 ms with a jobId and three seconds later read COMPLETED, processedItems: 1, problem: null with one JobResult, its report identical on both builds. The seven keys and their order are the same seven, verdict is still the stored status, and every row written for the pair went back out through DELETE /api/jobs/{id}. The receiver’s own count was 72 hits for 66 rows and 6 for a second subscription: no attempt was surplus and none lost, so the contract — one lead.created per row — was not what could be shed. Only the order.
src/lib/webhook-fanout.ts is the arithmetic with no fetch, no database and no clock in it, the same reason job-report.ts and export-stop.ts hold the reading and no query, and the reason the width is testable at all. fanOut(items, width, run) claims items by an index that only advances — nothing lost, nothing twice, a task that throws holds its slot rather than degrading the pool toward serial under exactly the load it exists to spread, a width above the item count costing one wave and not a promise per item. tallyDeliveries takes attempted from the outcomes rather than from a number the caller supplied, and keeps the three kinds of failure apart because they are three parties: refused (reachable, answered 500), no-answer (no HTTP response), blocked (this instance’s own egress policy refused the address before it was attempted) — collapsing them is what made a subscriber’s application bug and a typo in its URL look identical from outside. classifyDeliveryFailure orders its arms by measurement: egressCode first, so a policy refusal is never charged to a receiver; then TimeoutError, whose measured message here (The operation was aborted due to timeout) names no budget, so the number is stated rather than quoted; then the socket, measured as TypeError: fetch failed with the real sentence one level down in cause (connect ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:8123). dispatchWebhookEvent is now one call to dispatchWebhookBatch with a single row — one delivery path, not a second set of rules. The width (8) is spent per subscription, not across the batch, deliberately: nine endpoints on one event are nine independent receivers and must not cost two attempt budgets (5.029 s with two silent subscriptions against 5.028 with one, and 15.183 s for 20 rows across both against 15.072 s across one: the subscriptions are dispatched concurrently, so a second receiver is not a second budget charged to the caller’s wait), while the one receiver with 240 events waiting is posted to eight at a time instead of having 240 sockets opened at it.
Live on the shipped build, same receiver, same legs: 20 rows against a 300 ms receiver 6.222 → 0.987 s (⌈20/8⌉ × 0.3 = 0.9); 20 rows against a silent one 100.190 → 15.072 s (⌈20/8⌉ × 5 = 15); 40 rows 200.275 → 25.156 s; 5 rows 25.070 → 5.038 s (one wave); a single create 5.037 → 5.028 s, unchanged, because one row was already one wave; the no-subscription case 0.166 → 0.215 s, one findMany plus the new report key. Integrity: with the receiver restarted holding the subscription’s own secret, HMAC verified on every delivery under both header spellings and 21 distinct event ids for 21 deliveries — the id is per event, shared by that event’s subscriptions, never minted per delivery; the receiver was reset for it and then landed /black 21 and /black2 21 — each path’s 20 import rows plus that leg’s own one single create, which is not in attempted: 40 because the report counts only the batch it belongs to — so the waves lost nothing. The invisible failure is now in the body: "webhooks":{"subscriptions":1,"attempted":20,"delivered":0,"refused":20,…,"lastError":"500 Internal Server Error"}, "noAnswer": 20 with "no answer within 5000 ms" for a silent one, and the same keys at zero for a file with no subscriber — webhooks is in every return path of the import report, including the two that create nothing, because a sometimes-absent key is a key whose absence means three things. POST /api/leads still answers with the lead row and nothing else: #17 pinned that body, and it is the one ?fields= projects, so an extra key there is a projection question and not this round’s measurement. Offline 1012/1012 (978 on the base, 34 new in tests/unit/webhook-fanout.test.ts over 6 describes — peak in flight asserted against a real pool, the throwing task, the claim order, wavesFor pinned to the numbers the README quotes (20→3, 40→5, 240→30), the tally adding up and reporting the last failure in completion order, a 500 kept apart from an outage, the three measured error shapes driven through classifyDeliveryFailure, and the source pins), tsc clean, lint 0 errors / 13 warnings (unchanged from main), check:contract 8/8, check:llms-full unchanged, build exit 0; one main pin retargeted with the reason in the commit — webhook-dispatch.test.ts’s AbortSignal.timeout(<digits>) now reads the named constant, and the rule it protects (a bounded attempt, never an unbounded delivery) is asserted twice, on the constant and on the spend of it.
Said rather than implied: the door still waits — the fan-out is inline (src/lib/queue.ts has no webhook arm and #17 already declined adding one for a best-effort delivery), so a 400-row import against a dead receiver still costs ⌈400/8⌉ × 5 s = 250 s; what changed is the multiplier, the row count divided by the width instead of being the width; nothing is recorded per delivery — webhooks is the outcome of the fan-out the caller’s own request performed, not a delivery log, so an event a scheduled job emits still lives only in that worker’s stdout, and an operator who wants “did my subscriber get yesterday’s leads” needs a deliveries table, which is a different round’s decision; attempted counts sockets, not acknowledgements (a receiver that answers 200 and drops the body is delivered); a 500 is displayed, not fixed — one attempt per (row, subscription) is still the policy, and retries would break the “deduplicate on id” promise; the single-create door is still silent about its own failure by the pin above; and no demo step, for the reason #51 and #52 recorded — aw-deploy’s only open pair (#96, STEP 27) is CONFLICTING. A webhook’s signing secret was re-printed by every read door and no door could change it — now it is shown once, by the door that mints it — 25 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#82 OPEN at 621e674 + cf19cf0 + 1b9e5fd, a direct child of main at 65bd942): GET /api/webhooks and GET /api/webhooks/{id} handed back the HMAC credential a subscriber verifies LeadWorks deliveries with, on every press, forever — and so did PATCH, which is the one nobody would guess to look at. Measured on this round’s own runtime first: the base built in the worktree, served on :3341 over the session’s own database leadworks_r54 with a private Redis on 6393, booted with the documented ABET_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS=1 so a subscription could name a receiver on this box; every number curl’s own %{size_download} and %{time_total}, and no whole secret is printed in any log. POST /api/webhooks → 201, 8 keys, secret 64 chars: the legitimate showing. The same row read back: list 200, 299 B, plaintext secret in body; detail 200, 284 B — 285 once the row reads isActive:false, the one byte being false against true, not a nonce; and PATCH {isActive:false} 200, 285 B — an unrelated flip reprinting the credential. Three reads in a row returned 1 distinct value, byte-equal to the one the create door handed out, so this is the stored secret; a list of 6 subscriptions was 200, 1,734 B with 6 secrets, i.e. the leak scales with the crowd.
And no door could change it, which is what made the shape irreducible rather than merely untidy: PATCH naming only the secret → 400 "no field to update was sent" — refused without saying which field it refused, when one field was in fact sent and it is the one this door has never accepted (src/lib/fields.ts already names Webhook.secret as deliberately unwritable); PATCH naming it beside a legal field → 200, 287 B, the whole row with its plaintext secret, the caller’s chosen value ignored; POST with a caller-chosen secret → 201, the door mints its own. So the column is never accepted as input and printed as output, and the only replacement was DELETE + POST — a new id, a new createdAt, the subscriber’s endpoint told — while the old row kept serving the old value until it was deleted. Two things bound the damage and both are pinned rather than asserted: an anonymous read was closed by an earlier round (401 with no key; another tenant’s key → 200 empty list / 404 / 404 / 404), so this is a tenant’s-own-keys problem; and nothing in src/ verifies an inbound signature — both header spellings exist only at src/lib/webhooks.ts:89 and :95, on the sending side — so a caller holding the secret is not inside LeadWorks. What it can do is stand in front of LeadWorks to the subscriber, which is exactly the trust the signature exists to create. The projection asks confirm the leak was structural and not a filter that could be asked off: base GET …?fields=secret → 200, 323 B, 8 keys, secret printed (64 chars) and …?select=secret → 200, 308 B, the same — the door read no such parameter. Those two presses were taken on the still-serving #53 build on :3331 after checking that git diff --stat 65bd942 966ac9c -- src/app/api/webhooks src/lib/fields.ts returns nothing, so that instance’s webhook read doors are byte-identical to main’s.
The rule was already written twice in this product: src/lib/apikeys.ts:12 “Mint a key. The plaintext is returned once and never persisted.” and src/app/terms/page.tsx:20 “Plaintext keys are shown once — if you lose one, mint a replacement and revoke the old one.” A signing secret cannot honour the storage half of that rule: verifying a bearer token needs only its digest, while an HMAC delivery is signed with the value itself, so src/lib/webhooks.ts must keep reading it straight from the table. What can be decided is which door says it out loud — the fix is the mouth, not the column. src/lib/fields.ts gains WEBHOOK_READ_COLUMNS, every column of model Webhook except secret, plus webhookReadSelect(), living beside the input tables that already record the same fact from the other side so the two halves of one statement are on one screen; the two reads and PATCH’s answer pass that select (PATCH because an unrelated isActive flip was one of the three ways the value came back); POST /api/webhooks is untouched — 201, the row, the secret, the one moment the caller has never held it; and DELETE still answers { ok: true } and discards the row it removes.
Live on the shipped build, same instance, same receiver: list 299 → 223 B, detail 284/285 → 208 B, PATCH 285 → 209 B, 6 subscriptions 1,734 → 1,278 B with 0 secrets in any of them, while the read row still carries all seven columns an operator actually audits (id, workspaceId, url, events, isActive, createdAt, updatedAt). Delivery is the part a reader should expect to break, so it was measured: with the receiver restarted holding the created subscription’s own secret, one POST /api/leads produced 1 delivery, X-Leadworks-Signature verified 1 of 1, the generic sha256= spelling verified 1 of 1, 1 distinct event id; the row was then PATCHed naming a caller-chosen secret — ignored, as before — and a second lead still produced 1 delivery to /ok, verified with the secret the create door handed out. A PATCH {isActive:false} in between produced zero deliveries, which is isActive being honoured and not this change, so the next leg re-activates through the door and says so. The projection stays structural: ?fields=secret and ?select=secret now answer 200 with no secret, and the 401 / foreign-key answers are unchanged. Said rather than implied: the secret stays plaintext in the table — this changes which door prints it, not how it is stored, because the sender must have the value; there is no rotation door — replacing a secret is DELETE then POST, a new row, a new id, a new createdAt, and the subscriber’s endpoint told; a rotate action is a real gap and a different round’s decision, and hiding the value makes it sharper, which is why it is named here rather than left in the diff; a tenant’s own key still sees everything else about a subscription — url, events, isActive, both timestamps — and what it can no longer see is the one column that lets someone impersonate this product to a third party; keys are not scoped, so “whoever can read” is “anyone holding any live key of that workspace”, including a key handed to a read-only integration — revocation remains the boundary, this round only narrows what a key can print; the create response is the only durable copy, so an operator who loses it and logged nothing must recreate, which is the trade this round chose and the README states; and the OpenAPI document never named this column (measured: 0 paths naming a webhook secret), so the machine-readable contract is unchanged and check:contract passes without an edit. An egress guard that refuses 127.0.0.1 stored ten internal targets written in IPv6, and one delivery then fetched a second address nobody asked about — 26 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#83 OPEN at b42827f + 869943b + 90b7db7 + d2b9e8a + 04d9b1f, a direct child of main at 65bd942): src/lib/egress.ts judges an address — loopback, RFC1918, link-local, CGNAT, 169.254.169.254 — and every door hands it one through new URL(...), which rewrites an IPv6 literal into compressed hex on the way. Measured on this round’s own runtime first: the base built in the worktree, served on :3351 over the session’s own database leadworks_r55 with a private Redis on 6394, booted deliberately without ABET_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS so the guard ran at its documented default, against a loopback receiver on :8097 and a second listener on :8096. One listener, eight notations at POST /api/webhooks: http://127.0.0.1:8097/ok → 400, http://[::1]:8097/ok → 400, and six IPv6 coats of the same socket — [::ffff:127.0.0.1], [::ffff:7f00:1], [0:0:0:0:0:ffff:7f00:1], [::7f00:1], [64:ff9b::7f00:1], [2002:7f00:1::] — each stored with 201. Four more internal targets arrived coated the same way and were stored the same way (the metadata address as [::ffff:a9fe:a9fe], this box’s own 10.0.0.82 at :6379, a 192.168.4.5, a 100.64.0.1 mesh address), and POST /api/sources took the identical press in its config.url with a 201. The caller wrote [::ffff:127.0.0.1]; the door kept [::ffff:7f00:1]: the guard’s v4-in-v6 branch tested the text of a ::ffff:-prefixed dotted tail, and the parser had already turned that tail into two hex groups. Not a door-only defect — three of the six stored coats put a real signed lead.created (1337-byte body, evt_… id, both header spellings) on the loopback listener, an address every write door refuses when it is written the ordinary way, and the other three failed at the connect (TypeError: fetch failed, ENETUNREACH for 2002:7f00:1::), which is this box’s routing and not the policy’s opinion; the metadata row was deleted before a single lead existed, so what is claimed about it is the door’s verdict, not a socket. Why the suite was green: it pinned isInternalAddress("::ffff:127.0.0.1") — the bare string, the one spelling no caller produces. The second hole needed no parser: deliver()’s fetch followed 3xx by default, and a follow is a second address assertAllowedTarget was never asked about — measured as a subscription whose endpoint replied 302 with Location: http://127.0.0.1:8096/stolen, the dotted form the door refuses, which the delivery then requested; :8096 logged 1 hit, GET, 0 bytes, the body and event id dropped at the hop by the method change and X-Leadworks-Event carried through.
The fix reads the address, not the string. v6Groups() parses a literal into its eight 16-bit groups — folding a trailing dotted quad into the last two, returning null for one that does not parse — embeddedV4() returns the IPv4 that four forms carry inside them (v4-mapped, dotted or hex, compressed or expanded; v4-compatible ::x:y; the NAT64 well-known prefix 64:ff9b::x:y; 6to4 2002:x:y::), and internalV6() answers why a literal is internal as the address the refusal should name, so the decision and the explanation cannot drift; a literal that does not parse is refused rather than guessed at, in either family. A coat around a public IPv4 stays public: on the fixed build [::ffff:8.8.8.8]:53, [::ffff:0808:0808]:53, [64:ff9b::0808:0808] and [2002:0808:0808::] (all 8.8.8.8) and [::ffff:5d9b:d822] (93.155.216.34) are each 201 with the URL stored verbatim — the same range table read in the second notation, not a ban on the notation. The refusal says url must not be an internal address (::ffff:7f00:1 carries 127.0.0.1), both halves, because the caller wrote one and the parser sent the other, and it says neither for an address internal without carrying anything — [::1], [fe80::1], [fd12::1] name themselves. That sentence is a correction found on this round’s own first draft, which printed 0.0.0.1 for [::1]: true about the eight groups, false about the reason, and a message that sends an operator to a host nobody wrote. src/lib/webhooks.ts adds redirect: "manual", so a 3xx is left as the response it is and lands in the same non-2xx line a 500 does.
Live on the fixed build, same doors, same list: the ten coated subscriptions and the coated source press are eleven refusals, four forms the base run never reached refused too, the five public coats still 201, POST /api/sources refusing config.url in its own label with both halves — ten rows flipped and none of the admissible ones moved. The redirect leg had to be re-taken on a second boot with ABET_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS=1, because after this fix every address reachable on this box is internal and hop 1 can only be admitted by saying so: hop 1 logged 1 hit POST with the same Location, hop 2 logged no hit at all, and the outcome is one line in the app’s stdout — webhook … -> lead.created: 302 Found. The same boot delivered one ordinary signed event to a loopback subscriber and the receiver verified it under both header spellings (signatureMatches: true, genericMatches: true), which is the check that the redirect change did not disturb the path that still works; and the hatch said what it let through — allowed despite internal addresses ::ffff:7f00:1. A filter word is not a crowd — five places took an enum word straight off the request and answered 200 total=0 for a typo — 26 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#84 OPEN at e56df94 + cb59649 + 43a882e, a direct child of main at 65bd942): Lead.status, Job.type, Job.status and Export.status are plain String columns with their vocabulary in a schema comment, so Prisma neither validates nor case-folds them, and four query-string filters plus Export.filterJson.status handed the caller’s word straight to where. Measured on this round’s own runtime first — main built in the worktree, served on :3361 over the session’s own database leadworks_r56 with a private Redis on 6395, booted without ABET_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS because every press here is a query string and no socket is — over six leads one per Lead.status word, the five job types and one completed export. status=new → 200 total=0 while status=NEW → total=1; NEW%20 and %20NEW 0, the padding became the word; NEW,ENRICHED 0, a comma is not an OR; ACTIVE, DONE, PROCESSING, null 0 each; NEW&status=ENRICHED total=1 — one honoured, the other dropped in silence; jobs?type=scrape 0 while POST /api/jobs {type:"scrape"} at the same door is a 400; exports?status=PROCESSING 0 — a real Export.status value no file in src/lib/enums.ts declared; exports?status=completed 0 against COMPLETED 9. The same doors already refuse ?fields=nonsense naming 25 columns, and the write routes already spoke the sentence this round installed. The stored filter was worse than an empty page: POST /api/exports {filterJson:{status:"ACTIVE"}} and {status:"qualified"} were 201 → COMPLETED records=0 bytes=0 — a delivered file reporting itself a success — while the identical body at POST /api/lead-lists was a 400 naming the set.
Three behaviours meet here and this round changed the third: refuse (every validating write route), fold (the rule and list doors — {conditions:{status:"new"}} was a 201 whose stored row reads {"status": ["NEW"]} in the table afterwards) and ignore (the five above). src/lib/list-filter.ts reads every value with getAll, trims, drops empties, dedupes, and hands the door one checked word or the write routes’ sentence. Three decisions, each matching a rule the app already runs: padding is not part of the word; an empty value names nothing (?status= stays the whole page — a form whose select has no choice sends exactly that; contrast ?fields=, refused, an empty list of columns being a projection of nothing); a word repeated is a question with two answers (400 status was given 2 words ("NEW", "ENRICHED"); this filter compares status to one of them). EXPORT_STATUSES is declared for the reader, breaking enums.ts’s own rule with the reason beside it: POST /api/exports stores PENDING, exporter.ts:43 writes PROCESSING, then COMPLETED/FAILED, and no route writes the word — a declaration the app then caught being right, because no base press ever caught PROCESSING (every poll struck 3 s late and found COMPLETED) while this round’s 200 ms poll read poll 1: PROCESSING, poll 2: COMPLETED. Three limits are stated rather than left to luck: a range or a list is judged by its longest member (0 0 31 4-11 * and 0 0 31 1,4 * stay storable, and 0 0 1-31/30 2 * — which names the 1st and the 31st of February — stays because February has the 1st); the weekday is left out of the verdict (0 0 29 2 1 matches nothing in the scanned window and everything on 2044-02-29, a Monday — the pin asserts that date rather than arguing about it); and a cadence misfiring twice (23 59 30 2 *) is refused for its fields, because both sentences in one message would bury the complaint the caller can act on. Five LeadWorks doors answered a duplicate with conflict: {fields: []} — Prisma 7 put the collision in the index’s own name and nothing read it — 28 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#100 at 89e4013): conflict.fields is the one part of a 409 body a client can act on, the thing that marks an input box red, and on this app it was always empty. conflictFor read error.meta.target, and Prisma 7 with the pg driver adapter leaves that array present and empty while putting the collision in the constraint’s name — measured off the error on a live instance before anything was written (e3fcc16, fresh worktree, app on :3418 over the session’s own leadworks_r67, private Redis 6409, two workspaces, keys in 0600 files printed nowhere): {…,"constraint":{"index":"Lead_workspaceId_email_key"},"table":"Lead"}…,"modelName":"Lead","target":[]}. So five doors shared one blind answer (r67_press_base.log): the byte-identical POST /api/leads, the PATCH onto those same bytes, add-leads with a member already in the list, add-leads typing the same id twice in one call, and add-to-list on a membership that exists — each {"error":"That Lead already exists in this workspace.","conflict":{"fields":[]}}, the membership doors saying it about a table name with no field at all. Prisma names a unique index <table>_<column>[_<column>]_key (Workspace_slug_key, Lead_workspaceId_email_key, LeadListMember_listId_leadId_key, all three read out of pg_indexes), and the same error carries the table twice, so the columns are what sits between the prefix and the _key: src/lib/unique-index.ts does that split and nothing else. Four decisions, each with its reason — meta.target stays the authority when it carries something (the client’s own words beat Postgres’ words about the table); an empty array is not a target, and a check that only asked whether the key exists would answer “nothing collided” forever, which is what made this durable; no hand-written map of index names to columns, because a list falls behind the schema the day someone adds a unique column and then the door names the wrong thing with confidence — the split’s one assumption (an underscore means one thing) is instead proved from the schema text: every @unique/@@unique declared, its name rebuilt, must split back into the declared columns, so a future home_town or a map:-renamed constraint breaks a test rather than a caller; and when the name does not decompose the module says nothing, so the door keeps today’s nameless sentence — a wrong field marks the wrong input red. Measured on the running app first (ff38306 built in a fresh worktree, :3435 over this session’s own leadworks_r74, private Redis 6418, keys in 0600 files printed nowhere; 13 leads all at score=50, nine sources, nine jobs, nine exports and nine lists each sharing one createdAt, hand-written ids ld_r74_00…_12 so the tie is readable in /tmp/opencode/r74_press_ab.log): the leads walk at limit=3 over p1..p5 (total=13, totalPages=5) returned 13 rows in 12 distinct — ld_r74_00 on two pages, ld_r74_02 on none; ?limit=2 reached 11 of 13 while ?limit=4/5/7 reached all 13, so the page size decides which tied row is lost; GET /api/{sources,jobs,exports,lead-lists}?limit=2 answered 8 of 9 with one row repeated; and ?limit=3&page=1 (tied06,09,00) against the same URL with &fields=email,score (tied12,11,10) was one door, one page, two answers, disagreeing on all five pages — #47 and #48 had already made both branches share one pageWindow so they could not disagree about the filter, and they did share it; they each took a different slice out of a tie.
The silence goes two ways, which is what makes this the worst shape a read door can have: every number beside the rows vouched for an answer that was dropping them, and re-issuing the same URLs on trunk was stable — walk 2 of ?page=1..5 was identical to walk 1, and the walk after one ordinary PATCH was identical to the walk before. The loss lives between shapes of the same question, so no retry of one endpoint would have shown it, and the fix is a source-wide rule with a census over it. That rule is the app’s own, twice over: #45 ordered the membership window by leadId (unique within a list), and RULE_PRIORITY_ORDER (src/lib/rule-priority.ts) ends priority desc, createdAt asc, id asc so “the same two rules produce the same plan every minute”. src/lib/pagination.ts now carries that key as PAGED_TIEBREAK and the six orders a page is cut from end with it — orderBy: [{ createdAt: "desc" }, PAGED_TIEBREAK] at the four doors, [{ score: "desc" as const }, PAGED_TIEBREAK] inside the leads door’s shared pageWindow. No new parameter, no re-ranked default, no migration: the primary key is the one each door already used, and only the order inside a band of ties is now decided rather than left to the sort. Two doors’ silence in the map went with it, because a caller cannot walk a page they are not told about — GET /api/lead-lists pages with skip/take/totalPages and a default of 20 while documenting workspaceId alone, and GET /api/lead-lists/{id} returns memberPage and documented neither ?page= nor ?limit=; both carry them now, the six collection operations’ page line names the order its page is cut from, and public/llms-full.txt was rebuilt from the contract rather than edited — 8,447 bytes at ff38306, 8,531 here, one line gained — the list door’s summary — because that file carries summaries and auth, not query descriptions.

The reading rule is the app’s own and was already typed three times — boolParam (bool-word.ts:103) does getAll → trim → drop empties → dedupe → refuse above one word → judge, idFilterParam (ids.ts:145) reads the same way before resolving an id, short() (validation-engine.ts:450) trims before quoting. src/lib/action-word.ts is that rule for the six doors that act, with their vocabularies beside it (SOURCE_ACTIONS, JOB_ACTIONS, EXPORT_ACTIONS, LEAD_ACTIONS, LEAD_LIST_ACTIONS, RULE_ACTIONS) and three stated decisions: padding is not part of the word, an empty value names no action (a door that has to do something has no default to fall back on), and a word repeated is one word while two different words are a question with two answers — refused, both named, in the caller’s own order. Each door’s refusal is emitted on the line where that door already answered Unknown action — below the lookup and ownership check at jobs/exports/lead-lists, above it at sources/leads/enrichment-rules — so no door’s auth order moved and no status code moved: every 400 stays a 400, the 409s, 404s and the retired-file 410 are untouched, and no door gained or lost an action.

A cancelled export stops — the mark outranks the file, its bytes come back, and the same retry door runs the crowd again — 23 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#67 at b9db97d — LANDED by rebase-proxy as #68 · main now 8a8805d — and aw-deploy#85 at 561a207 — LANDED the same way as #87 · main now 7b520d1 — each branch a direct child of its repo’s main, which already carried the landed #41 and #42, so neither diff carries another round’s commits and the review is of the hashes named): #41 wrote down the two workers that do not consult the mark and left them; #42 made a stop undoable and said a stopped export was still not resumable. Measured on the running app before this round, that was worse than silence: ?action=cancel on a queued EXPORT job answered 200 {“ok”:true}, at +97 ms the pair read Job[CANCELLED 0 0] beside Export[COMPLETED /tmp/export_1792669709914.pdf 19], and at +236 ms Job[COMPLETED 19 19] — the operator’s mark erased in the 139 ms between by the worker’s own two writes, export.completed already delivered for a file its operator had cancelled, and ?action=retry refusing the row because it did not say FAILED. An export’s indivisible unit of work is the file, not a lead: one crowd query, rendered in memory, written once — so a stop is before the bytes or after them, and the second shape leaves what the row-wise workers never do: bytes on a disk no row will ever name. src/lib/export-stop.ts owns both halves and holds no query, no unlink, no status code: the record goes down FAILED — one of the four words Export.status’s own comment documents, so no fifth word was invented — quoting the cancel, with filePath/fileSize/expiresAt/completedAt nulled and recordCount: 0; nulled rather than omitted, because this write can land on a row the same run had already stamped COMPLETED, and a row that says FAILED while still pointing at bytes being deleted is a door answering about a file the disk has not. FAILED is exactly the word the retry door accepts, so a stopped export is resumable from the moment it stops. Bytes are reclaimed through planRowDelete — the same guard DELETE /api/exports/{id} asks — which keeps “we only delete files we wrote” one rule with three callers (the sweep on a date, the delete door on a row, a stopped worker on its own bytes) instead of a second copy that can drift, and keeps none an honest third answer. In the worker: a guarded claim (status: { not: MARK }, count: 0 and it never begins), the mark asked at the three boundaries this work has, a guarded finish, the put-down in the order disk → record → job, and export.completed dispatched only as the try’s last statement.

Then ?action=retry on that row → 200 and COMPLETED, 12,019 rows in 616,087 bytes, statSync equalling the row’s fileSize, a lifetime of exactly the 25,000 ms this instance exports with, served at 200, 1 delivery. The before-the-bytes shape is made deterministic, not raced: export concurrency is 2, so two exports hold both slots and a third is cancelled 1,728 ms after creation with its job provably PENDING — the claim refused, FAILED beside CANCELLED 0+0 of 0, nothing unclaimed in its window, and DELETE answering {“ok”:true,“file”:“no file”}, which is the answer #37’s order can only give because this round nulled the pointer.
