Said out loud in the README and the demo’s own stdout, not hidden: a stop landing while the crowd query is open is only seen at the boundary after it (which is why totalItems is the whole crowd with nothing processed); a stopped export has no partial arithmetic to keep; a file the guard will not name stays on the disk and is counted by #37’s census; lib/scraper.ts still drives through a stop, and adding a fifth obeying worker means changing that census rather than drifting past it; two rows about one file are still two rows — no transaction, just the order and the guarded writes; and no history was rewritten. The step is self-correcting and its reader cannot re-derive why: a psql spawn costs ~1.2 s on this box against a 4.2 s run, so the read immediately before the cancel is GET /api/jobs/{id}, and a cancel that arrives after the finish makes the step re-aim at 0.42 of the measured duration instead of passing silently.
A deleted export takes its file with it, and the files with no record are counted out loud — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#48 OPEN at 6da319b + d7bb9a3, stacked on #46 · aw-deploy#62 OPEN at 30f9f08 + fccaa21, stacked on #61): the round before this one called the 24 files no row names DELETE /api/exports/{id}’s work and pinned that the door “removes the row and only the row”. Measuring that was the round’s first job, and the attribution did not survive it: Job rows of type EXPORT number 60 against 29 Export rows, and all 31 jobs naming an exportId with no row were created inside the single hour 2026-10-17 07:00, 24 of them COMPLETED — the same 24, in the same five minutes (07:40–07:45) the orphan mtimes occupy. The demo never calls DELETE on an export and its first export code landed ~3 h after that window, no test calls the four writers, and three sampled orphans have atime == mtime (xfs relatime, /tmp not its own mount: a download bumps atime, never mtime). So the residue is job history from a window in which the table was emptied, and the door’s measured contribution here is zero rows — a fact about the data, not a defence of the code: the door would have left the file, and until this PR it did.
The row goes first, because the two failures are not equal. planRowDelete(filePath) is the same isExportArtifact question planPurge asks, with the same two words for saying no (no file, path is not an export artifact) and deliberately no clock: expiresAt governs the doors that serve, never the record, so an export one second from its expiry is still deleted the moment its caller asks. DELETE /api/exports/{id} reads filePath in the same select that authorizes it, checks ownership, deletes the row, then acts on the plan — because a row outliving its file is a download door pointing at nothing (the state the operating system used to produce by reclaiming /tmp, and the reason a second 410 sentence exists at all) while a file outliving its row is what /tmp already holds. Every answer after the delete names the bytes: removed, already gone from this server (an ENOENT is a fact about /tmp, neither a deletion nor a failure), left on disk with the error’s own message beside it, or the guard’s reason — and the bare { ok: true } over a file that is still there is gone from the door, with a test counting its four NextResponse.json exits and refusing the fifth. The other half is counted, not deleted: logExportFileCensus(now) rides the same minute and prints the export-shaped files no row names when the number moves, because such a file has no expiry, no status and — decisively — no workspaceId, so there is no policy to apply to it and no tenant whose authorization could cover removing it. It sets aside anything whose own filename millisecond is younger than a minute: the gap between a name and its row’s completedAt, measured across the 25 live rows, is 2.3–16.1 ms, so a sweep that deleted what no row names would be deleting exports being born. The count goes to the server’s stdout and never onto an API — a test scans every file under src/app/ to keep that true.

A retired export’s bytes are reclaimed too — by the minute, on its own date, and only files the worker can prove it wrote — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#46 OPEN at 97548ae + d505a2b, stacked on #45 · aw-deploy#61 OPEN at d1b8f43 + 3e9fe00, stacked on #59): the round before this one shut both download doors on Export.expiresAt and said out loud that it had not touched the file. Measured against the running app the same day, that limit was load-bearing: 27 COMPLETED rows, 2 stamped and both past their date, with both files still on disk byte-exact (/tmp/export_1792571592233.csv at 4,195 bytes — the row says 4,195 — and export_1792571761063.csv at 957); 51 files matching /tmp/export_* against those 27 rows, so 24 are named by no row at all, which this callout called the work of DELETE /api/exports/{id} and the round below measures away — they are an hour of job residue from a table that was emptied, and the door had never been walked in this database; 0 rows whose file was missing, so the door’s missing-file 410 had never fired here and never could; and grep over src/ — four sites write a /tmp/export_<millis>.<ext> and zero delete anything. The application had never removed a file it created, so 16 leads’ contact rows sat in /tmp behind a door that had already told its caller to re-run the export.
A date is the whole authority, so the decision asks it first. planPurge(rows, now) in src/lib/export-retention.ts is pure — no disk, no database, no clock of its own — and calls the same retentionVerdict the doors use before it looks at the path, which is what makes “a purge cannot change what a door answers” true by construction rather than by luck: every file this reclaims was already refused by the row’s own date when the row was read. Then that a path is named at all, then that it matches what the four writers can actually produce — /tmp/export_<digits>.<csv|json|xlsx|pdf> — and everything else is kept with one of four reasons an operator can read back: served, unstamped, no file, path is not an export artifact. The guard is checked before the first unlink this application has ever attempted, because the path being deleted comes out of a database column and a column can hold anything — a row edited by hand, a future writer that stores a caller-supplied name, a ../../ typed into psql. It accepts nothing today that the worker does not write and the test proves that by lifting the four template literals out of exporter.ts rather than remembering them. purgeRetiredExports(now) in src/lib/scheduler.ts is the first unlink in src/ (the second arrived with #37, at the door that deletes a row, consulting the same guard): at the top of the once-a-minute sweep inside its own try/catch so a filesystem that misbehaves cannot cost the jobs that minute is due for; its where clause is only a pre-filter and the same now is handed to planPurge, so a bug in the query deletes nothing; it unlinks only plan.remove’s own filePath; an ENOENT is counted as already gone rather than reported as a deletion or a failure; and every removal is one stdout line naming the row id, the path and the date that authorized it. The row is not rewritten — filePath, fileSize and COMPLETED all stay, because the row is the record of an export that happened and the date on it is what says the bytes are gone; which is also why both doors still answer the retired sentence after the sweep instead of the lost-file one. 626/626 unit tests (17 new; and #45’s own “nothing deletes the bytes yet” test retargeted rather than deleted — the invariant it pinned was that the worker and the doors do not destroy what they serve, and they still do not), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors / 18 pre-existing warnings, build exit 0. Honest limits: an unstamped row keeps its file — null is a lifetime nobody promised, not an expiry that passed, and the same reasoning that keeps those 25 rows served keeps them on disk (backfilling stays an operator decision with an UPDATE in it); the 24 orphans stayed, because reclaiming a file whose record is gone needs a rule about what such a file means and a decision about DELETE /api/exports/{id} — delete the bytes with the row, or refuse to delete the row while its file is served — so this round counted them and a test pinned that nothing changed; nothing is deleted at the door itself (serving and destroying stay separate code paths); and no page shows an operator what the sweep took — the evidence is the row’s date, the door’s sentence and the server’s stdout. Merge order: #46’s evidence is the stamp and the two doors #45 landed, so land #42 → #55 → #43 → #56 → #44 → #58 → #45 → #59 → #46 → aw-deploy#61 → #48 → aw-deploy#62; STEP 17 fails against an older leadworks build on purpose, because there the refused export’s file survives every minute forever, which is exactly the state this round ends. 626/626 and 173 ✓ / 0 ✗ are that build’s numbers; the suite is at 670 and the run at 207 checks from #37 and #38 on.
The date an export’s file stops answering is on the row, and both doors obey it — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#45 OPEN at fcb7a5d + 1f93026, stacked on #44 · aw-deploy#59 OPEN at 4e11321 + d6238e1, stacked on #58): schema.prisma:315 has declared expiresAt DateTime? on Export since init_postgres, every export door has handed the key back, and nothing ever filled it and nothing ever read it. Measured against the running app first: POST /api/exports came back COMPLETED 1130 ms later with a real completedAt, a real filePath, fileSize=918 and recordCount=16 — and expiresAt: null; both downloads answered 200; the table held 25 COMPLETED exports and 0 with an expiry stamped, the oldest finished four days earlier with its file still on disk and still served (200, 1528 bytes); none of the 11 prisma.export.* mutations (3 creates, 7 updates, 1 delete) passed the column, and not one of the 29 expiresAt mentions outside src/generated/ was about Export. Meanwhile the only place the word “expired” lived in the whole app was the download door’s own 410 — “export file expired from this server — re-run the export” — which fires when stat/readFile throws, i.e. when the operating system happened to reclaim /tmp: a sentence about expiry with no date behind it anywhere, and a file the machine lost indistinguishable from a row that never had a lifetime.
src/lib/export-retention.ts does the two things that make the column true and no more. expiryFor stamps the lifetime in the export worker’s single COMPLETED update, beside the completedAt from the same finishedAt — the row that names the file is the row that says how long it lives, and a scan over every call body in src/ counts 10 create/update writes and demands exactly one carry expiresAt. retentionVerdict → served / retired / unstamped, boundary inclusive, is asked by both doors that hand the file out — GET /api/exports/{id}/download and POST /api/exports/{id}?action=download — before either opens it, so a retired export is refused by policy rather than by whatever /tmp did, and the URL door can no longer hand out a link to a file the same module refused. retiredExportError and MISSING_EXPORT_FILE_ERROR live beside the verdict as two sentences a test keeps apart. The lifetime is EXPORT_RETENTION_MS, default 604800000 (seven days), read when the export finishes — which is what makes a short value safe: it belongs to the row and is stamped by the instance that wrote the file, so the demo’s 25 seconds cannot retire an export a seven-day instance finished earlier. Anything that is not a run of digits falls back to the default instead of becoming NaN in a Date ("1e9", "1500.5", "-5000", "5,000"; "007" → 7; "0" legal and meaning what it says). null is served, not refused — 25 real rows were finished before the column was ever filled, and retiring them on the strength of a column nobody wrote would be a policy decided by a migration. Callers still cannot name a lifetime: EXPORT_CREATE takes four keys and neither export door has a PATCH verb. 609/609 unit tests (36 new in tests/unit/export-retention.test.ts), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors / 18 pre-existing warnings, build exit 0. Live, demo STEP 16 — 11 checks, whole run 164 ✓ / 0 ✗ against that build (the run against #46 is 173 ✓ / 0 ✗, above), the runner booting only that app at EXPORT_RETENTION_MS=25000 because a proof that needs a week is not a demo: created with expiresAt: null, finished 08:36:01.064Z stamped 08:36:26.064Z — +25000 ms exactly — then 200 with all 957 bytes and its URL handed out; 25 seconds later both doors answer 410 in the same words, export retired on 2026-10-21T08:36:26.064Z — re-run the export for a fresh file, while the row still reads COMPLETED with the same filePath and fileSize, and the export finished before the round (expiresAt: null) still downloads 200. Honest limits: a retired export is a closed door, not deleted bytes — this round stops serving the copy, and the unlink plus the sweep that would call it are the next one, which is why the suite scanned for no unlink in any file that round touched, rather than trusting the prose — #46 retargeted that scan instead of deleting it: the worker and both doors still delete nothing, and the sweep is where the deletion moved to; the stamp lands only on rows finished from here, so an already-COMPLETED export keeps its null unless an operator backfills it with an UPDATE of their own; two instances with different settings produce rows with different lifetimes by design; and no page renders a countdown — the doors return expiresAt and the refusal quotes it. Merge order: #45 sits on #44’s branch and shares its CI trigger problem (--base main), so land #42 → #55 → #43 → #56 → #44 → #58 → #45 → #59 → #46 → aw-deploy#61; STEP 16 fails against an older leadworks build on purpose — there the finished row keeps expiresAt: null and both doors keep serving the file, which is exactly what this round ends.
Which rule owns a column, when two of them want it in the same minute — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#44 OPEN at d0e0110 + 865cc5a, stacked on #43 · aw-deploy#58 OPEN at 1ad15c7 + 021adc7, stacked on #56): EnrichmentRule.priority Int @default(0) was stored, accepted at both rule doors and returned to whoever asked, and nothing ordered by it. Measured against the running app first — one lead, three SCHEDULED rules over it each asking seniority, created in the order 10, 90, 50: the list door returned 50, 90, 10 (orderBy: { createdAt: "desc" }, the number the operator set in none of it); the sweep queued them 10, 90, 50 (its findMany carried no orderBy at all — heap order was the rule order); and the rule that filled the column was the one queued last, prio=50 logging SUCCESS ["seniority"] while prio=10 and prio=90 logged PARTIAL [] with beforeData {"seniority":"C_LEVEL"} — because the enrich worker runs concurrency: 5, so the three jobs raced for one lead and the first write landed. Two sweep minutes later that one lead carried six log rows, each recording creditsUsed: 1, five of them refusals that were guaranteed before any provider woke up.
An orderBy would not have made the ranking true — reordering a race still leaves the race to decide the column. What a ranking can decide is who owns a column, and that has to be settled before anything is queued. src/lib/rule-priority.ts settles it: RULE_PRIORITY_ORDER (priority desc, createdAt asc, id asc — a total order, one constant quoted by the list door and both sweeps, so what an operator reads and what the minute does cannot drift, and paging stops being a coin toss on a same-millisecond pair) and planTick, which walks one workspace’s due rules highest-ranked first and lets each claim the columns its own actions.fields asked for; the next rule in line keeps only what is unclaimed, and a rule left with nothing queues not at all — no job, no log row, nothing recorded against creditsUsed, no lastEnrichedAt stamp. Claiming is by intent, decided before any provider runs, so the lower-ranked rule’s job carries ["companySize"] rather than the two it asked for: narrowing at the queue, the one place a concurrent worker cannot undo it. Both sweeps walk the one plan, ?action=run stays deliberately unranked (an operator naming a rule outranks every number in the table), and fields.ts validates priority as an int in 0–100 at both doors, importing the bounds from the module that walks the ranking so the refusal and the walk cannot disagree. 573/573 unit tests (27 new in tests/unit/rule-priority.test.ts, plus five source scans in #42/#43’s files retargeted to read the same invariants through planTick rather than deleted), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors / 18 pre-existing warnings, build exit 0. Live, demo STEP 15 — 24 checks, whole run 153 ✓ / 0 ✗, exit 0: three rules created 50, 90, 10 come back from the door as 90, 50, 10 while their stored createdAts say the opposite; the sweep minute that follows, 57 s in, queues two jobs for three due rules — prio=90 with config.fields=["seniority"], prio=10 with ["companySize"], prio=50 with no job and no log row — and the lead ends seniority=C_LEVEL, ENRICHED, 47 → 53 with #43’s beforeData {"seniority":null} beside the winner; then the number moves — priority: 200 answers 400 priority must be at most 100, priority: 95 is stored, and the next minute hands ["seniority","companySize"] to the rule at 95 and starves the two that used to outrank it. Ownership followed the number, both minutes, which is why the step waits on two real sweep minutes instead of pressing a button. Honest limits: a starved rule leaves no row — the cost shows as the absence of a job and of a creditsUsed, and the sentence explaining it is in the server’s stdout, not in EnrichmentLog; the two visits that remain are still two visits, since a rule left a remainder nobody can answer (derive reads titles, not company sizes) still logs a PARTIAL and spends a creditsUsed, and no plan written before the provider runs can know that; ownership is a minute and a workspace, not a lease — recomputed each sweep, per tenant, and two rules over disjoint columns still both run; a rule whose actions name no columns claims all 18, because “the rule did not say” means “whatever the provider answers”; and no page renders the ranking yet — the door returns priority, and the list now arrives in its order. Merge order: #44’s evidence is the beforeData half #43 stores and the pruning #42 lands, so land #42 → #55 → #43 → #56 → #44 → #58; STEP 15 fails against an older leadworks build on purpose, because there the three rules each queue a job and the column goes to whichever worker gets there first.
What the row said, before a visit changed it — 21 Oct (PRs aw-leadworks#43 OPEN at b047411 + 38b87ec, stacked on #42 · aw-deploy#56 OPEN at e8c187f + 50a863d, stacked on #55): schema.prisma pairs EnrichmentLog.beforeData Json? with afterData Json? — the half for what the row said and the half for what it became. Only the second was ever written. Measured against the running app first: the lead whose rep-typed VP beat the provider’s C_LEVEL (the column holds, so the write plan refuses the answer) and the lead whose rule declared fields: ["industry"], so the seniority the provider answered was pruned before the plan saw it, left byte-identical log rows — ["PARTIAL", [], null, {}, "derive"] — while the facts behind them are the two opposite ones (“the row’s own word won” against “nobody asked for that”). The third lead, the gap that was filled, carried beforeData: null as well, so a refusal, a pruning and a filled gap were one silence.
planEnrichmentWrite now returns before, keyed by what was answered rather than by what was written: the row is asked before it is changed, including for the columns this visit is about to refuse. updateLeadEnrichment stores it as beforeData in the same single enrichmentLog.create that writes fieldsEnriched and afterData — one plan per visit, one row per plan, so the two halves can never describe different visits. null is the row saying it held nothing, and enrichment is additive, so the first visit to an empty column logs {"seniority": null} beside C_LEVEL and the second logs {"seniority": "C_LEVEL"} beside {}; a value the plan refuses to coerce (["React"] in the row against a bare "React" from the provider) is recorded as the row had it while afterData stays empty; a pruned column never reaches the plan, so it is absent; and logEnrichmentError invents neither half, because nothing was answered. 546/546 unit tests (18 new in tests/unit/enrich-provenance.test.ts), tsc clean, eslint 0 errors / 18 pre-existing warnings, build exit 0. Live, demo STEP 14 — 10 checks, whole run 129 ✓ / 0 ✗, exit 0: the VP row logs beforeData {"seniority":"VP"} where the pruned row logs {}, after the step shows the two still agreeing on status, the empty write list and the empty afterData — the byte-identical pair, split; the gap logs null beside C_LEVEL with SUCCESS, ENRICHED and 47 → 53; the typed word survives with status still NEW; and derive reading Head of Growth and abstaining still records the visit — a stamp, a PARTIAL, nothing in either half. Honest limits: an abstaining provider and a pruned answer are still one record — empty beforeData both times, because nothing reached the plan — and what separates them is the rule’s own actions, which that step prints above each row, not anything the log invents; explain-lead still cites only afterData; and while the lead’s detail door serves both halves whole, no page renders either half yet. Merge order: #43 needs #42 at runtime, not merely in history (the pruned case exists only because restrictToDeclared prunes it) and STEP 14 reads actions.fields off the rule — land #42 → #55 → #43 → #56.
The API now answers at the path crawlers look for — 18 Oct (PR aw-abetapi#14): the contract was already generated from code at /api/v1/openapi; /openapi.json now re-exports that same handler, so BlueRails and API tooling find a contract instead of a 404, and the two paths cannot diverge. Verified on the production build: both 200 application/json, byte-identical, OpenAPI 3.1.0 v1.1.0, 16 paths; the index lists both. The acceptance sweep now fails if the alias 404s or drifts (rehearsed: identical body PASS, a different version at each path FAIL).
The lead tool can finally take the list the buyer already has — 18 Oct (PR aw-leadworks#22 MERGED): the landing page promised “CSV uploads” while the repo only had an exporter. The importer now parses header aliases (E-mail, Company Name, LinkedIn URL), comma/semicolon/tab files, quoted commas, escaped quotes, BOM/CRLF/CR-only and multi-line fields — with real line numbers, so a quoted newline cannot shift later errors. Every row is judged by the same LEAD_CREATE table as POST /api/leads (no second validator); duplicates are skipped within the file and against the workspace, case-insensitively by email; and POST /api/leads/import accepts text/csv or JSON { "csv": "..." } (1,000 rows / 5 MB). The dashboard gets a session-only card — the browser never needs an API key, and the workspace comes from the session. Measured through the production build on Postgres: a 4-row file → 201, imported 2, duplicate line 3, invalid line 4; the same file again → 200, 0 imported, 2 existing; phone-only and bare-LinkedIn rows normalized (+14155550100, https://linkedin.com/in/erin); no key 401, foreign workspace 403, csv: 42 400, a header with no contact column 422; dashboard 200 with the form, and a real 1.15 MB / 1,000-row upload through the dashboard’s server action → 200, “Imported 1000 of 1000 rows” (the action’s cap now matches the importer; the framework default was 1 MB). The homepage now shows the measured four-row response beside the signup CTA, instead of the word “CSV” in a feature card; the card shows the first five problems and downloads all 50 as a formula-safe line,field,message CSV, so the fix happens in the file the buyer already has. 305 tests (22 new, including the exporter's own column names, a HubSpot-shaped row, and the formula-safe problems report), tsc + build clean. This is the buyer’s first real action in week one, and it is now backed by code rather than a sentence.
On the call, same day (PR aw-deploy#21 MERGED, after #22): demo/run-demo.sh leadworks now imports a four-row CSV as STEP 7 — 201 / 2 imported / 1 duplicate / 1 invalid, the bad email on line 5, the re-import 200 / 0 / 2 already present, the two imported rows among the 10/10 signature-verified deliveries, and the 22-row file the product exported re-importing with 0 new rows (22/22 already present, 0 invalid); a failed claim sets exit 1 instead of printing and passing.

04 · How to achieve it

The roadmap — fix, sell, get cited, grow

Ordered by dependency: nothing sells until the origins answer; nothing gets cited until the site lives on its real domain; nothing scales until each repo is public, billed and deployed. The website build itself is done — it needs shipping, not building.

1

Fix the foundation — the P0 list

This week
🔴Fix abetworks.in (HTTP 520). The apex domain of the business is down and 70 references point at it. Fastest path: put the modern Render site on the real domain — deploy it to Vercel (free tier) or a paid Render instance and attach abetworks.in + www. Keep the onrender URL as the demo.
🔴Deploy the hub so hub.abetworks.in answers. One deploy of aw-abethub (or one HUB_URL env var set correctly family-wide) fixes 175 canonical/og:image references and every social card in the 35-tool family. Until then every deployed copy publishes a canonical to an unreachable host — search engines drop those pages.
✅Push the platform apps — DONE (16 Oct, 06:37–06:46 UTC). The held-back repos were flipped public and pushed; the app clones got origins. What remains: fast-forward the local checkouts (bash verify-apps-sync.sh --pull — all clean, behind origin 1–4) and turn on Dependabot alerts + secret scanning + push protection on the newly public repos. Note: local aw-abetanalytics sits on a fresh feat/stripe-billing branch with no commits ahead of main yet — Stripe billing is the next milestone.
✅Flip the 10 private repos to public — DONE 16 Oct. All are public now; the mission rule (work only on public repos) is satisfied family-wide. Keep the security guards on for each newly public repo (Dependabot + secret scanning + push protection).
🟡Decide the wildcard story: point *.abetworks.in at the hub/product apps, or stop advertising <slug>.abetworks.in anywhere. Wildcard DNS exists but answers 520.
2

Make the first products sellable

Weeks 2–4 · then repeat per repo
✅aw-abetanalytics: Stripe billing — done. Merged #10 (checkout · portal · webhook · trial nudges), plus Prisma 7 + Postgres switch on main. The five revenue apps all bill (analytics $19 · agent $99 · leadgenius $29/user · leadworks $49 · Connect $24/agent, 14-day trials); abetflow + abetapi are deliberately $0 suite infrastructure. First dollar = Stripe products + keys — ready-to-paste CLI in stripe-setup.md.
✅aw-leadworks: upgrades merged — Prisma 7, bullmq 6 + ioredis 6, Postgres switch all on main; Stripe was already wired. Still open: xlsx 0.20.3 security bump (PR #15).
🔌nuCRM integration — inbound half done + outbound landing on main: abetapi's verified, deduped, fanned-out inbound bridge merged (#11); both sides are Postgres now (nuCRM Drizzle, apps Prisma). Outbound subscriptions now exist in the paid fleet — leadgenius, leadworks and agent on main, and aw-abetanalytics #14 (MERGED) adds the last missing one: signed snapshot.created + alert.triggered deliveries under both X-Abetanalytics-Signature and the generic X-Webhook-Signature: sha256=… an inbound nuCRM plugin verifies natively. Remaining: converge UI patterns. The apps already expose API-key machine auth — that's the ready surface.
🚀Deploy = one blueprint import now: render.blueprint.fleet.yaml (PR aw-deploy#3) creates all 7 services + datastores + domains wired together (≈$91/mo stated upfront; $0 trial profile included), each app self-migrating on boot. Live URL + subscriptions = the mission's "sellable MVP" definition.
✅Copy audit on the tool family (P1 truth gap) — done, PR aw-abethub#7. All 35 widget cap-notes, ToolShell (FAQ, upsell, structured data), the index and the terms now write paid-tier claims as planned — nothing on the hub pretends to be purchasable; JSON-LD offers exist only for the free tools. A new copy-truth.test.mjs guard bans the present-tense "X paid does Y" shape fleet-wide, so the rot cannot return silently. Registry prices remain the planned catalogue — real prices are still the owner's call.
🔁Repeat per repo as they're flipped public, in the owner's order: the 2 public MVPs first, then the rest. Keep the parity guard (check-parity.mjs) passing: hub ↔ standalone forks must stay identical.

Convert & grow

After launch stabilises

🧪Keep the "run it, don't claim it" rule from the audit: every number published should be produced by running something — where it can't be, say UNVERIFIED.

05 · House rules

Rules already set — keep them

Repo discipline (nuCRM house style)

  • Never push to main
  • Branch: fix/<description> · PR base: main
  • One issue per PR
  • Run postman/full-test-suite.sh before pushing
  • Dependabot + secret scanning + push protection ON for every repo

Mission discipline (owner set)

  • Every repo = sellable MVP: live SaaS with subscriptions
  • Compatible with nuCRM, yet fully standalone
  • Only work on PUBLIC repos — flip private ones first
  • Integrate over HTTP API/webhooks, never shared DB
  • Custom "All rights reserved (Abetworks)" license is intentional (not SPDX) — no change needed
